\chapter{Etymologisches Wörterbuch: The Comparative Lexicon}
This chapter catalogs the surviving roots of the Eurasian-Dravidian macro-family. The strict application of the Glottalic Shift Law and the naso-laryngeal principles ensures that these are genetic inheritances, not loans.

\begin{longtable}{p{0.15\textwidth} p{0.15\textwidth} p{0.15\textwidth} p{0.15\textwidth} p{0.25\textwidth}}
\toprule

\textbf{*NaR-} & *Nar- & *Nar- & *Nar & to see/perceive \\ 
\multicolumn{5}{p{\textwidth}}{\small The PED root \textbf{*NaR-} meaning 'to see/perceive' presents a classic example of the Glottalic Shift. In the Western branch, the ejective N loses its glottalic burst, surfacing as the voiced stop N in PIE \textit{*Nar-}. This is heavily attested in the archaic strata of Vedic and Greek. In Dravidian, the root surfaces as \textit{*Nar-}, maintaining the voiceless quality but losing the glottal stricture, a hallmark of the Southern continuum. Finally, Proto-Sino-Tibetan heavily aspirates the initial consonant to yield \textit{*Nar}. The preservation of the final sonorant/fricative R across all three families confirms the extreme antiquity of this conceptual cluster, likely dating to the Upper Paleolithic.} \\ \addlinespace

In Dravidian, the root surfaces as \textit{*tel-}, maintaining the voiceless quality but losing the glottal stricture, a hallmark of the Southern continuum. Finally, Proto-Sino-Tibetan heavily aspirates the initial consonant to yield \textit{*thel}. The preservation of the final sonorant/fricative L across all three families confirms the extreme antiquity of this conceptual cluster, likely dating to the Upper Paleolithic.} \\ \addlinespace
